can be used in combination with perturbation bounds and entropic continuity bounds like those derived e.g. in
[
69
,
68
,
62
,
61
]
to control the deviation of energy-constrained channel capacities in presence of a Lindbladian perturbation. Since these considerations go beyond the scope of the present paper, we do not pursue them further. Instead, we provide a basic illustration of the method we propose in the case of Gaussian perturbations of Gaussian semigroups by combining Proposition
5.1
with Lemma
5.5
.

Corollary 5.6

Let
(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{qOU}},\mathcal{T}_{f})
be the generator of the quantum Ornstein Uhlenbeck semigroup with
\lambda>\mu\geq 0
and
(\varepsilon\mathcal{L}_{G},\mathcal{T}_{f})\coloneqq(\varepsilon\mathcal{L}[{\gamma a+\eta a^{\dagger}}],\mathcal{T}_{f})
a Gaussian perturbation with
\gamma,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varepsilon>0
. Then, assuming
\lambda^{2}-\mu^{2}+|\gamma|^{2}-|\eta|^{2}>0
as in Proposition
5.1
, there exist uniformly bounded functions
C(\varepsilon)
,
D(\varepsilon)
such that, for all
t\geq 0
:

(86)

\Big\|e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{qOU}}}-e^{t(\mathcal{L}_{\operatorname{qOU}}+\varepsilon\mathcal{L}_{G})}\Big\|_{\diamond}^{E}\leq\,(1+E)\varepsilon\,C(\varepsilon)\,\max\Big\{1,D(\varepsilon)\Big\}\,.

6
Discussion and open questions

In this paper, we proved a generation theorem for bosonic Sobolev preserving quantum dynamical semigroups, which we also extended to time-dependent evolutions on bosonic multi-mode systems. The property of Sobolev preservation allowed all-time perturbation bounds, which we later applied to bosonic Gaussian semigroups, the quantum Ornstein Uhlenbeck semigroup, and dissipative CAT-qubit dynamics. As a consequence, we also established convergence results for the adherent points in the limit of large times. For this, we only required two physical assumptions on the generator (i) that it is in GKSL form with a Hamiltonian and jump-operators defined by polynomials in the annihilation and creation operators; and (ii) a bound on the output moments of the generator in terms of its input-moments. An interesting question for further work would be to further generalize the result by allowing different Sobolev spaces on which the semigroups are defined, and by this enlarge the class of generators covered by the generation theorem. In this line of research, a more detailed consideration of the Toffoli gate would be interesting.

7

The authors have no further relevant financial or non-financial interests to disclose.

Data availability statement:

No datasets were generated or analyzed during the current study.

Appendix A
Semigroup perturbation theory

Here, we prove Theorem
2.11
stated in the preliminary section.

Theorem A.1

Let
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
and
(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K}))
be two generators of
C_{0}
-semigroups on
\mathcal{X}
, for an operator
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{K}))
. Moreover, let
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W}))
be an invertible operator on
\mathcal{X}
with bounded inverse, such that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W})
is an
\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K}
-admissible subspace (see Definition
2.9
) and such that
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{W}^{-1}
is bounded. Then, for all
x\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W})
,

\|(e^{t\mathcal{L}}-e^{t(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq t\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{W}^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}}\;\int_{0}^{1}\|e^{(1-s)t\mathcal{L}}\|_{\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}}\;\|e^{st(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})}\|_{\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}}\;ds\|x\|_{\mathcal{W}}.

and especially

(e^{t\mathcal{L}}-e^{t(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})})x=t\int_{0}^{1}e^{(1-s)t\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{K}e^{st(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{K})}x\;ds.

Proof.

For simplicity, we assume that
t=1
. To be able to use the integral form, we start by considering the following vector-valued map:

[0,1]\ni s\mapsto e^{(1-s)\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{K}e^{s(\mathcal{L}+\varepsilon\mathcal{K})}x.

It is clear that
s\mapsto e^{(1-s)\mathcal{L}}
is a strongly continuous map of bounded operators. Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{K}e^{s(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}x-\mathcal{K}e^{(s+s^{\prime})(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{W}^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\,\|(e^{s(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}x-e^{(s+s^{\prime})(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}x)\|_{\mathcal{W}}

shows that
s\mapsto\mathcal{K}e^{s(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}
is strongly continuous because
s\mapsto e^{s(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}
defines a
C_{0}
-semigroup on
(\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{W}})
. Then, for every converging sequence
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\rightarrow s
for
n\rightarrow\infty
the set

\left\{\mathcal{K}e^{s_{n}(\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K})}x\,|\,n\in\mathbbm{N}\right\}

is relatively compact in
\mathcal{X}
. Therefore, the strong continuity of
s\mapsto e^{(1-s)\mathcal{L}}
is equivalent to uniform continuity by
[
23
, Prop. A3]
, the considered vector-valued map is continuous and we can use the fundamental theorem of calculus for the generalized Riemann integral so that

\|(e^{\mathcal{L}}-e^{\mathcal{L}+\mathcal{K}})x\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\varepsilon\int_{0}^{1}\|e^{(1-s)\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{K}e^{s(\mathcal{L}+\varepsilon\mathcal{K})}x\|_{\mathcal{X}}ds

proves the theorem.
∎

Appendix B
Bosonic single mode system

In this section, we prove some basic properties of polynomials of annihilation and creation operators. We shortly repeat the normal form of our polynomials in
a
and
a^{\dagger}
given in
Equation

14
:

p(a\,,a^{\dagger})=\sum_{i+2j\leq\deg(p)}\lambda_{ij}(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}+\,\sum_{k+2l\leq\deg(p)}\mu_{kl}N^{l}a^{k}

with coefficients
\lambda_{ij},\mu_{kl}\in\mathbbm{C}
. The modification considered in our proof (see Lemma
3.2
) is given in
Equation

16
by

\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})\coloneqq N^{2d}+p(a,a^{\dagger})\,.

Then, we start proving a simple representation of a domain of
p(a,a^{\dagger})
and
\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})
which extends the domain
\mathcal{H}_{f}
: For
n\in\mathbbm{N}

(87)

\displaystyle p(a\,,a^{\dagger})\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i+2j\leq d}\lambda_{ij}n^{j}\sqrt{i!\binom{n+i}{i}}\ket{n+i}+\sum_{k+2l\leq d}\mu_{kl}(n-k)^{l}\sqrt{k!\binom{n}{k}}\ket{n-k}

where
d\coloneqq\deg(p)
. This directly implies for
|\phi\rangle=\sum_{n}\phi_{n}|n\rangle\in\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2})

\displaystyle p(a\,,

\displaystyle a^{\dagger})\ket{\phi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i+2j\leq d}\phi_{n}\lambda_{ij}n^{j}\sqrt{i!\binom{n+i}{i}}\ket{n+i}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k+2l\leq d}\phi_{n}\mu_{kl}(n-k)^{l}\sqrt{k!\binom{n}{k}}\ket{n-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i+2j\leq d}\left(\phi_{n-i}\lambda_{ij}(n-i)^{j}\sqrt{i!\binom{n}{i}}+\phi_{n+i}\mu_{ij}n^{j}\sqrt{i!\binom{n+i}{i}}\right)\ket{n}\,.

Then, the leading order of the summands in
n
is maximal of order
d/2
so that
\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2})
is a domain of
p(a,a^{\dagger})
. For the modified polynomial
\tilde{p}
, there is sequence of functions
R_{n}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
with asymptotics strictly smaller than
n^{4d}
such that

(88)

\|\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})\ket{\phi}\|^{2}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}|\phi_{n}|^{2}n^{4d}+R(\phi)\,.

Having the domain above in mind, we can prove that
p
is closable and
\tilde{p}
is a closed operator with core
\mathcal{H}_{f}
:

Lemma B.1

(Adjoint and core of polynomials of
a,a^{\dagger}
)

Let
p\in\mathbbm{C}[X,Y]
be a polynomial on
\mathbbm{C}
and
(p(a,a^{\dagger}),\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2}))
the unbounded operator in normal form (
15
). Then,
p(a,a^{\dagger})
is closable and there is a
c\geq 0
such that for all
\ket{\phi}\in\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2})

\|p(a,a^{\dagger})\ket{\phi}\|\leq c\|(\mathbbm{1}+N)^{d/2}\ket{\phi}\|\,.

The modification
\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})=N^{2d}+p(a,a^{\dagger})
is closed with
\mathcal{D}(\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger}))=\mathcal{D}(\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})=\mathcal{D}(N^{2d})
and core
\mathcal{H}_{f}
.

Proof.

First, note that the relative boundedness w.r.t. the number operator is a direct consequence of
Equation

87
.
To prove that
p(a,a^{\dagger})
is closable, we show that
\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2})\subset\mathcal{D}(p(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})
: we recall that the adjoint is defined via boundedness of the functional

\mathcal{D}(p(a,a^{\dagger}))\ni\ket{\phi}\mapsto\braket{p(a,a^{\dagger})\phi\,,\varphi}

for
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(p(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})
. Since for all
n,m\in\mathbbm{N}

\braket{a\,n\,,m}=\delta_{n,0}\delta_{n-1,m}\sqrt{n}=\delta_{n,m+1}\sqrt{m+1}=\braket{n\,,a^{\dagger}\,m}

and by the definition of the domains, we know that for all
\ket{\phi},\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{D}(N^{k+\frac{l}{2}})

\braket{N^{k}(a^{\dagger})^{l}\phi\,,\psi}=\braket{\phi\,,a^{l}N^{k}\psi}\quad\text{and}\quad\braket{a^{l}N^{k}\phi\,,\psi}=\braket{\phi\,,N^{k}(a^{\dagger})^{l}\psi}\,.

By sesquilinearity of the scalar product and
Equation

15
, the above equations hold for all polynomials
p\in\mathbbm{C}[X,Y]
which proves
\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2})\subset\mathcal{D}(p(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})
. Since
\mathcal{D}(N^{d/2})
is a dense subspace of
\mathcal{H}
, Theorem 7.1.1 in
[
64
]
shows that
p(a,a^{\dagger})
is closable. Next, we show that the modified polynomial

(N+\mathbbm{1})^{2d}+p(a,a^{\dagger})

is already closed. Actually, we show
\mathcal{D}(N^{2d})=\mathcal{D}(\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})
, i.e. the domain is maximal, by contradiction. Assume that there exists a
\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})\backslash\mathcal{D}(\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger}))
and define
P_{M}
to be the projection on the first
M
Fock basis elements. Then, we use the representation in
Equation

14
so that, denoting by
\tilde{p}^{\dagger}
the polynomial where we took the complex conjugate of the coefficients and swapped the coordinates,

P_{M}\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})=P_{M}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{2d}P_{M}+P_{M}\left(\sum_{i+2j\leq d}\overline{\lambda}_{ij}N^{j}a^{i}P_{M-i}+\,\overline{\mu}_{ij}(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}P_{M+i}\right)P_{M+d}\,.

We then can define the state

\ket{\phi_{M}}\coloneqq\frac{P_{M}\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})\ket{\varphi}}{\|P_{M}\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})\ket{\varphi}\|}\in\mathcal{H}_{f}

Next, we use
\phi_{M}
to get a lower bound on the operator norm of

\mathcal{D}(p(a,a^{\dagger}))\ni\ket{\phi}\mapsto\braket{\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})\phi\,,\varphi}

by

\displaystyle\sup_{\|\phi\|=1}|\braket{\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})\phi\,,\varphi}|

\displaystyle\geq|\braket{\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})\phi_{M}\,,\varphi}|=|\braket{\phi_{M}\,,P_{M}\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})\varphi}|=\|P_{M}\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})\varphi\|.

Now, by definition of
\tilde{p}
and denoting by
\varphi_{n}
the coefficients of
|\varphi\rangle
in the Fock basis,

\|P_{M}\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})\varphi\|^{2}=\Big\|\sum_{n=0}^{M+d}\varphi_{n}P_{M}\left((N+\mathbbm{1})^{2d}+p^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})\right)\ket{n}\Big\|^{2}\,,

where
p^{\dagger}
is defined similarly to
\tilde{p}^{\dagger}
. By assumption
\phi\notin\mathcal{D}(N^{2d})
, so that the above sequence is diverging for
M\rightarrow\infty
to infinity (see
Equation

88
). This contradicts the assumption and shows
\mathcal{D}(\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger})=\mathcal{D}(N^{2d})
as well as
\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=\tilde{p}^{\dagger}(a,a^{\dagger})
. Moreover,
p(a,a^{\dagger})
is by Theorem 7.1.1 in
[
64
]
a closed operator. Since
\{\ket{n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
is an orthonormal basis and
N
a multiplication operator on that basis, we can immediately conclude that
\mathcal{H}_{f}
is a core for
N
and further for all
(\mathbbm{1}+N)^{k}
,
k\geq 0
. Since
\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})
is closed w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}(N^{p/2+1})
,
\mathcal{H}_{f}
is also a core of
\tilde{p}(a,a^{\dagger})
.
∎

Having in mind that polynomials of the annihilation and creation are closed operators on certain domains, we use the canonical commutation relation
[a,a^{\dagger}]=\mathbbm{1}
in the following lemma:

Lemma B.2

Let
l\in\mathbbm{N}
, then the following hold on
\mathcal{H}_{f}
and can be extended to maximal domains by taking limits

(89)

\displaystyle(a^{\dagger})^{l}a^{l}

\displaystyle=(N-(l-1)\mathbbm{1})(N-(l-2)\mathbbm{1})\cdots(N-\mathbbm{1})N\,,

\displaystyle a^{l}(a^{\dagger})^{l}

\displaystyle=(N+\mathbbm{1})(N+2\mathbbm{1})\cdots(N+(l-1)\mathbbm{1})(N+l\mathbbm{1})\,.

Proof.

The above equalities can be proven by induction over
l\in\mathbbm{N}
. The cases
l\in\{0,1\}
are trivial by definition. Next assume that the equation holds for
l\in\mathbbm{N}
, then by
Equation

49

\displaystyle(a^{\dagger})^{l+1}a^{l+1}

\displaystyle=(a^{\dagger})^{l}Na^{l}=(N-l)(a^{\dagger})^{l}a^{l}=(N-l)\cdots N

which finishes the proof by induction. The second expression can be proven by induction as well, and the induction start is again clear by the CCR. Next, we assume the equation for
l\in\mathbbm{N}
. Then,
Equation

49
shows

a^{l+1}(a^{\dagger})^{l+1}=a^{l}(N+\mathbbm{1})(a^{\dagger})^{l}=a^{l}(a^{\dagger})^{l}(N+(l+1)\mathbbm{1})=(N+\mathbbm{1})\cdots(N+(l+1)\mathbbm{1})

which completes the induction.
∎

Lemma B.3

Let
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbbm{N}
with
\min\{\ell_{1},k_{1}\}=\min\{\ell_{2},k_{2}\}=0
,
z\in\mathbbm{C}
, and
h:\mathbb{N}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
a positive function that is increasing in each of its variables. Then,

\displaystyle za_{1}^{\ell_{1}}a_{2}^{\ell_{2}}h(N_{1},N_{2})(a^{\dagger}_{1})^{k_{1}}(a^{\dagger}_{2})^{k_{2}}+\overline{z}a_{1}^{k_{1}}a_{2}^{k_{2}}h(N_{1},N_{2})(a^{\dagger}_{1})^{\ell_{1}}(a^{\dagger}_{2})^{\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2|z|\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}(N_{1}+m_{1}I,N_{2}+m_{2}I)\,,

where
m_{1}\coloneqq\max\{\ell_{1},k_{1}\}
,
m_{2}\coloneqq\max\{\ell_{2},k_{2}\}
and

\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}(n_{1},n_{2})={\prod_{j=n_{1}-m_{1}+1}^{n_{1}}\sqrt{j}\prod_{i=n_{2}-m_{2}+1}^{n_{2}}\sqrt{i}}\,\,\,h(n_{1},n_{2})\,1_{n_{1}\geq m_{1}}1_{n_{2}\geq m_{2}}\,,

where we introduced the notation
1_{x\geq m}
for the indicator function on the set
\{x:\,x\geq m\}
, and where by convention we take
\prod_{i=a}^{b}=1
when
a>b
.

Proof.

We define
K\coloneqq za_{1}^{\ell_{1}}a_{2}^{\ell_{2}}h(N_{1},N_{2})(a^{\dagger}_{1})^{k_{1}}(a^{\dagger}_{2})^{k_{2}}+\overline{z}a_{1}^{k_{1}}a_{2}^{k_{2}}h(N_{1},N_{2})(a^{\dagger}_{1})^{\ell_{1}}(a^{\dagger}_{2})^{\ell_{2}}
and represent it in the
2
-mode Fock basis:

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\,h(n_{1},n_{2})\,\big(za_{1}^{\ell_{1}}a_{2}^{\ell_{2}}\,\outerproduct{n_1,n_2}{n_1,n_2}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{k_{1}}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{k_{2}}+\overline{z}a_{1}^{k_{1}}a_{2}^{k_{2}}\outerproduct{n_1,n_2}{n_1,n_2}(a_{1}^{\dagger})^{\ell_{1}}(a_{2}^{\dagger})^{\ell_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\geq m_{1}\\
n_{2}\geq m_{2}\end{subarray}}g_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\\
k_{1},k_{2}\end{subarray}}(n_{1},n_{2})

\displaystyle\left(z\outerproduct{n_1-\ell_1,n_2-\ell_2}{n_1-k_1,n_2-k_2}+\overline{z}\outerproduct{n_1-k_1,n_2-k_2}{n_1-\ell_1,n_2-\ell_2}\right)\,,

where

\displaystyle g_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\\
k_{1},k_{2}\end{subarray}}(n_{1},n_{2})

\displaystyle\coloneqq h(n_{1},n_{2})\sqrt{n_{1}\dots(n_{1}-\ell_{1}+1)n_{1}\dots(n_{1}-k_{1}+1)n_{2}\dots(n_{2}-\ell_{2}+1)n_{2}\dots(n_{2}-k_{2}+1)}\,.

By assumption, since
\min\{\ell_{1},k_{1}\}=\min\{\ell_{2},k_{2}\}=0
, we have that
g_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\\
k_{1},k_{2}\end{subarray}}(n_{1},n_{2})=\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}(n_{1},n_{2})
for
n_{1}\geq m_{1}
,
n_{2}\geq m_{2}
, and

\displaystyle K=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}}\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}(n_{1}+m_{1},n_{2}+m_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left(z\outerproduct{n_1+k_1,n_2+k_2}{n_1+\ell_1,n_2+\ell_2}+\overline{z}\outerproduct{n_1+\ell_1,n_2+\ell_2}{n_1+k_1,n_2+k_2}\right)\,.

Next, we consider the constituents of the above sum individually. Note that the operator

(90)

\displaystyle z\outerproduct{n_1+k_1,n_2+k_2}{n_1+\ell_1,n_2+\ell_2}+\overline{z}\outerproduct{n_1+\ell_1,n_2+\ell_2}{n_1+k_1,n_2+k_2}

\left(\begin{array}[]{*{6}{c}}\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}\right)\,.

can be embedded into an operator on a two-dimensional space of the form

z\outerproduct{e_1}{e_2}+\overline{z}\outerproduct{e_2}{e_1}\,,

where
\ket{e_{1}}
and
\ket{e_{2}}
are orthonormal vectors. For
\ket{e_{1}}=\ket{e_{2}}
,
z+\overline{z}\leq 2|z|
shows

z\outerproduct{e_1}{e_2}+\overline{z}\outerproduct{e_2}{e_1}\leq|z|(\outerproduct{e_1}{e_1}+\outerproduct{e_2}{e_2})\,.

In the case
\ket{e_{1}}\neq\ket{e_{2}}
, we have

Eigenvalue

Eigenvectors

|z|

\ket{\psi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}|z|}(|z|\ket{e_{1}}+z\ket{e_{2}})

-|z|

\ket{\varphi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}|z|}(|z|\ket{e_{1}}-z\ket{e_{2}})

.

so that

z\outerproduct{e_{2}}{e_1}+\overline{z}\outerproduct{e_1}{e_{2}}=|z|\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}-|z|\outerproduct{\varphi}{\varphi}\leq|z|\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\leq|z|(\outerproduct{e_1}{e_1}+\outerproduct{e_2}{e_2})\,.

This allows us to estimate

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbbm{N}}\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}(n_{1}+m_{1},n_{2}+m_{2})|z|(\outerproduct{n_1 + k_1, n_2 + k_2}{n_1 + k_1, n_2 + k_2}

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt+\outerproduct{n_1 + l_1, n_2 + l_2}{n_1 + l_1, n_2 + l_2})

\displaystyle\leq 2|z|\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}(N_{1}+m_{1}I,N_{2}+m2I)

employing the monotonicity of
\widetilde{h}_{m_{1},m_{2}}
in both arguments in the last step.
∎

Appendix C
Inequalities for power functions

Many bounds in Sections
4
and
5
can be deduced from bounds on real-valued functions acting on the spectrum of the number operator
N
. Especially, the following functions, first introduced in
Equation

51
, will require special attention: Let
l,k\in\mathbbm{N}
,
f(x)=(x+1)^{k/2}1_{x\geq-1}
, and

(91)

g_{l}(x)=\begin{cases}f(x)-f(x-l)&x\geq l-1;\\
f(x)&l-1>x\geq 0;\\
0&0>x\,.\end{cases}

Lemma C.1

Let
g_{l}
be defined in
Equation

91
for
l,k\in\mathbbm{N}
. Then, for all
k\geq 2
and
x\in\mathbbm{R}

(92)

\displaystyle g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle\leq g_{l+1}(x)\,,

(93)

\displaystyle g_{l}(x-l)

\displaystyle\leq g_{l}(x)\,.

Proof.

By the monotonicity and non-negativity of
f(x)=(x+1)^{k/2}1_{x\geq-1}
,

\begin{cases}x\geq l-1&f(x)-f(x-l)\\
l-1>x\geq 0&f(x)\\
0>x&0\end{cases}=g_{l}(x)\leq g_{l+1}(x)=\begin{cases}f(x)-f(x-(l+1))&x\geq l\\
f(x)&l>x\geq 0\\
0&0>x\end{cases}\,,

which proves Inequality
92
. For Inequality
93
, we consider the following cases:

\begin{cases}f(x-l)-f(x-2l)\\
f(x-l)\\
0\\
0\end{cases}=g_{l}(x-l)\leq g_{l}(x)=\begin{cases}f(x)-f(x-l)&\qquad x\geq 2l-1\\
f(x)-f(x-l)&\qquad 2l-1>x\geq l-1\\
f(x)&\qquad l-1>x\geq 0\\
0&\qquad 0>x\end{cases}\,.

For
x<l-1
, the inequalities are clear by the non-negativity of
f
. The case
l-1\leq x<2l-1
follows by

2\frac{f(x-l)}{f(x)}=2\left(1-\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{k/2}\leq 2\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k/2}=1

and the last case
x\geq 2l-1
follows by monotonicity of
g_{l}
:

\frac{2}{k}g_{l}^{\prime}(x-l)=(x-l)^{k/2-1}-(x-2k)^{k/2-1}\geq 0\,.

∎

Next, we prove upper and lower bounds for
g_{l}
:

Lemma C.2

Let
g_{l}:\mathbbm{R}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}_{\geq 0}
be defined in
Equation

91
for
l\in\mathbbm{N}
. Then, for all
x\in\mathbbm{R}
and
k\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\begin{cases}x\geq l-1&(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}-1_{k\geq 3}(x+1)^{k/2-2}\,\,\frac{(kl)^{2}}{8}\\
x\geq l-1&(x+1)^{k/2-1}l\\
l-1>x\geq 0&(x+1)^{k/2}\\
0>x&0\end{cases}\leq g_{l}(x)

and

g_{l}(x)\leq\begin{cases}\frac{kl}{2}\left(1+1_{k=1}\right)(x+1)^{k/2-1}&x\geq 0\\
(x+1)^{k/2}&x\geq 0\\
0&0>x\end{cases}\,.

Proof.

The case
k=0
is trivial. We start with the upper bounds. By monotonicity of
g_{l}
, it is enough to prove the first upper bound just for
x\geq l-1
. For
k=1
,

g_{l}(x)=(x+1)^{-1/2}\frac{l}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{-1/2}ds\leq(x+1)^{-1/2}\frac{l}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s\right)^{-1/2}ds=(x+1)^{-1/2}l.

For
k\geq 2
,

g_{l}(x)=\frac{k}{2}\int_{0}^{l}(x+1-s)^{k/2-1}ds\leq\frac{kl}{2}(x+1)^{k/2-1}\,,

which finishes the proof of the first upper bound. The other two bounds are obvious by definition. Next, we consider the lower bounds. The case
x<l-1
is trivial so we are left with proving

(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}-\delta_{k\geq 3}(x+1)^{k/2-2}\frac{(kl)^{2}}{8}\leq g_{l}(x)

for
x\geq l-1
. For
k=1
, the integral representation can be lower bounded as

\displaystyle g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{-1/2}\frac{l}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{-1/2}ds\geq(x+1)^{-1/2}\frac{l}{2}\,.

For
k=2
, it is again easy to calculate the quantity
g_{l}(x)=l
, and for
k=3

\displaystyle g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{1/2}\frac{3l}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s_{1}\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{1/2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{1/2}\frac{3l}{2}-(x+1)^{-1/2}l^{2}\frac{3}{4}\iint_{0}^{1}s_{1}\left(1-s_{1}s_{2}\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{-1/2}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle\geq(x+1)^{1/2}\frac{3l}{2}-(x+1)^{-1/2}l^{2}\frac{3}{4}\iint_{0}^{1}s_{1}\left(1-s_{1}s_{2}\right)^{-1/2}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{1/2}\frac{3l}{2}-(x+1)^{-1/2}\frac{l^{2}}{2}.

Finally, the case
k\geq 4
is given by

\displaystyle g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s_{1}\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{k/2-1}ds_{1}

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}-(x+1)^{k/2-2}l^{2}\frac{k(k-2)}{4}\iint_{0}^{1}s_{1}\left(1-s_{1}s_{2}\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{k/2-2}ds_{2}ds_{1}

\displaystyle\geq(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}-(x+1)^{k/2-2}l^{2}\frac{k(k-2)}{4}\int_{0}^{1}s_{1}ds_{1}

\displaystyle\geq(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}-(x+1)^{k/2-2}\frac{(kl)^{2}}{8}\,

which proves the first non-trivial lower bound for
x\geq l-1
. Next, we consider

g_{l}(x)\geq(x+1)^{k/2-1}l\,.

The inequality is obvious for
k<2
by the same idea as before and for
k\geq 2

\displaystyle g_{l}(x)

\displaystyle=(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s_{1}\frac{l}{x+1}\right)^{k/2-1}ds_{1}

\displaystyle\geq(x+1)^{k/2-1}\frac{kl}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\left(1-s_{1}\right)^{k/2-1}ds_{1}

\displaystyle\geq(x+1)^{k/2-1}l

which ends the proof.
∎

Lemma C.3

Let
l\in\mathbbm{N}
and
x\geq l
, then

\displaystyle(x+1)^{l}-\frac{(l+1)l}{2}(x+1)^{l-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle((x+1)-l)\cdots((x+1)-1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(x+1)^{l}

\displaystyle(x+1)^{l}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(x+1)\cdots(x+1+(l-1)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle l!(x+1)^{l}

Proof.

To prove Lemma
C.3
, we redefine
y=x+1
and rewrite the first product as

p_{l}(y)\coloneqq(y-l)\cdots(y-1)\eqqcolon y^{l}-\frac{(l+1)l}{2}y^{l-1}+r_{l-2}(y)

where
r_{l-2}
is a polynomial of degree
l-2
. The proof idea is to show that
r_{l-2}(y)
is non-negative for all
y\geq l+1
, which proves the inequality. The non-negativity of the polynomial
r_{l-2}
can be proven by induction over
l
: The statement is directly clear for
l=1
and
l=2
. Next, we assume that
r_{l-2}
is non-negative for all
x\geq l+1
and show that
r_{l-1}
is for all
x\geq l+2
.

\displaystyle p_{l+1}(y)

\displaystyle=(y-(l+1))p_{l}(y)

\displaystyle=(y-(l+1))\left(y^{l}-\frac{(l+1)l}{2}y^{l-1}+r_{l-2}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=y^{l+1}-\frac{(l+1)(l+2)}{2}y^{l}+\frac{(l+1)^{2}l}{2}y^{l-1}+(y-(l+1))r_{l-2}(y)

\displaystyle=y^{l+1}-\frac{(l+1)(l+2)}{2}y^{l}+r_{l-1}(y).

For the second product
(x+1)\cdots((x+1)+l-1)
the lower bound is clear and the upper bound follows by

(x+1)(x+2)\cdots((x+1)+l-1)=(l-1)!\left(\frac{x}{1}+1\right)\cdots\left(\frac{x}{l}+1\right)\leq l!(x+1)^{l}.

∎

Appendix D
Technical lemmas for the quantum Sobolev spaces

Lemma D.1

(Continuity of
G(z)
)

Let
k_{0}<k_{1}\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
and
T:W^{k_{j},1}\to W^{k_{j},1}
, be a linear map with
\norm{T}_{W^{k_{j},1}\to W^{k_{j},1}}\leq M_{j}
, bounded by
M_{j}\geq 0
for
j=1,2
respectively. Further let
\theta\in[0,1]
and
k_{\theta}=(1-\theta)k_{0}+\theta k_{1}
and
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, then the map

\displaystyle G:S

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbbm{C}\>:\;0\leq\real(z)\leq 1\}\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto G(z)=(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}T\Big((N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k_{\theta}-k(z)}{4}}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k_{\theta}-k(z)}{4}}\Big)(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}

with
k(z)=(1-z)k_{0}+zk_{1}
, is well-defined, uniformly bounded and continuous.

Proof.

To prove the claim, we decompose
G
using the following auxiliary functions:

(94)

\displaystyle G_{1}:S\times W^{k_{1},1}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}

\displaystyle(z,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}

and

(95)

\displaystyle G_{2}:S

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}_{f}\subset W^{k_{1},1}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k_{\theta}-k(z)}{4}}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k_{\theta}-k(z)}{4}}\,.

We clearly have that
G_{1}(z,\cdot):W^{k_{1},1}\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
is a bounded linear map for all
z\in S
, since

(96)

\norm{G_1(z, y)}_{1}=\norm{(N + \1)^{\frac{\Re(k(z))}{4}} y (N + \1)^{\frac{\Re(k(z))}{4}}}_{1}\leq\norm{(N + \1)^{\frac{k_1}{4}} y (N + \1)^{\frac{k_1}{4}}}_{1}=\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

where we used that
k_{0}\leq\real(k(z))\leq k_{1}
and
(N+\mathbbm{1})^{i\frac{\imaginary(k(z))}{4}}
is a unitary that can be absorbed into the norm. Next, we will show that
G_{1}(\cdot,y):S\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
is continuous for all
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
. For that first note that, for
y\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, the claim follows directly from the continuity of
z\mapsto(n+1)^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}
with
n\in\mathbbm{N}
as a map from
S
to
\mathbbm{C}
. This is because all the involved operators can be considered finite dimensional using a cut-off of the Fock-basis. For a general
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
, we find
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{f}
, s.t.
y_{n}\to y
in
W^{k_{1},1}
, hence for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}

\displaystyle\norm{G_1(z, y) - G_1(z_0, y)}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{G_1(z, y - y_n)}_{1}+\norm{G_1(z, y_n) - G_1(z_0, y_n)}_{1}+\norm{G_1(z_0, y_n - y)}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{G_1(z, y_n) - G_1(z_0, y_n)}_{1}+2\norm{y - y_n}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\norm{y - y_n}_{W^{k_{1},1}}\,,

where we used
Equation

96
. Taking the limit
n\to\infty
concludes the claim that
G_{1}(\cdot,y):S\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
is continuous for all
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
. We further have that
G_{2}
as a map from
S
to
W^{k_{1},1}
is continuous, since
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
and the maps
z\mapsto(n+1)^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}
for
n\in\mathbbm{N}
are continuous as maps
S\to\mathbbm{C}
. This suffices since
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, hence all involved operators can be made finite dimensional via a cut-off in the Fock-basis again.

We can now write

G(z)=G_{1}(z,T(G_{2}(z)))

where
T(G_{2}(z))\in W^{k_{1},1}
as
T:W^{k_{1},1}\to W^{k_{1},1}
and
G_{2}(z)\in\mathcal{T}_{f}\subset W^{k_{1},1}
for all
z\in S
. This not only gives us that
G
is well-defined but also allows us to get

\displaystyle\norm{G(z)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\norm{G_1\left(z, T(G_2(z))\right)}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{T(G_2(z))}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{T}_{W^{k_{1},1}\to W^{k_{1},1}}\norm{G_2(z)}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{T}_{W^{k_{1},1}\to W^{k_{1},1}}\norm{(N + \1)^{\frac{k_\theta- k_0}{4}} x (N + \1)^{\frac{k_\theta- k_0}{4}}}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

where we again used
Equation

96
, giving us a bound independent of
z
. Further, using again
Equation

96
we can conclude continuity, since

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{G(z) - G(z_0)}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{G_1\left(z, T\left\{G_2(z) - G_2(z_0)\right\}\right)}_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{G_1\left(z, T\left(G_2(z_0)\right)\right) - G_1\left(z_0, T\left(G_2(z_0)\right)\right)}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{T}_{W^{k_{1},1}\to W^{k_{1},1}}\norm{G_2(z) - G_2(z_0)}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\lim\limits_{z\to z_{0}}\norm{G_1\left(z, T\left(G_2(z_0)\right)\right) - G_1\left(z_0, T\left(G_2(z_0)\right)\right)}_{1}

\displaystyle=0

where in addition we used the continuity of
G_{1}(\cdot,y):S\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
for
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
and
G_{2}:S\mapsto W^{k_{1},1}
.
∎

Lemma D.2

(Differentiability of G(z))

Let
k_{0}<k_{1}\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
,
T:W^{k_{j},1}\to W^{k_{j},1}
, be a linear map with
\norm{T}_{W^{k_{j},1}\to W^{k_{j},1}}\leq M_{j}
, bounded by
M_{j}\geq 0
for
j=1,2
respectively. Further let
\theta\in[0,1]
and
k_{\theta}=(1-\theta)k_{0}+\theta k_{1}
and
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, then the map

\displaystyle G:S

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbbm{C}\>:\;0\leq\real(z)\leq 1\}\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto G(z)=(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}T\left((N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k_{\theta}-k(z)}{4}}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k_{\theta}-k(z)}{4}}\right)(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}

with
k(z)=(1-z)k_{0}+zk_{1}
, is holomorphic on
\mathring{S}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbbm{C}\;:\;0<\real(z)<1\}
.

Proof.

To prove the claim, we follow a similar strategy as with Lemma
D.1
. We will again use the auxiliary functions
Equation

94
and
Equation

95
. We begin by showing that for a fixed
y\in W^{k,1}
,
G_{1}(\cdot,y):S\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
is holomorphic on
\mathring{S}
and initially even simplify to the case
y\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
. In this setting, all operators involved can be assumed to be linear maps on a finite subspace, by just taking a cut-off in the Fock-basis as we did before. This allows us to Taylor expand around
z_{0}\in\mathring{S}

\displaystyle(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z)}{4}}=G_{1}(z_{0},y)+G_{1}^{\prime}(z_{0},y)(z-z_{0})+\int\limits_{[z_{0},z]}G_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\omega,y)(\omega-z_{0})\,d\omega

where the integral is a path integral along the line segment
[z_{0},z]
and

\displaystyle G_{1}^{\prime}(z_{0},y)

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{0}-k_{1}}{4}\,\left(\log(N + \1)(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z_{0})}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z_{0})}{4}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.+(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z_{0})}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(z_{0})}{4}}\log(N + \1)\right)

and

\displaystyle G_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\omega,y)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{k_{0}-k_{1}}{4}\right)^{2}\left(\log^{2}(N+\mathbbm{1})(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+2\log(N + \1)(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}\log(N + \1)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\left.(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}y(N+\mathbbm{1})^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}\log^{2}(N+\mathbbm{1})\right)

are linear in
y
. From this representation, we can immediately deduce holomorphy of
G_{1}(\cdot,y):S\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
at
z_{0}\in\mathring{S}
and hence on all of
\mathring{S}
. To lift holomorphy from
y\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
to
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
, we note that for
z_{0}\in\mathring{S}
there exists
C_{z_{0}}\geq 0
such that for
y\in\mathcal{T}_{f}

(97)

\norm{G'(z_0, y)}_{1}\leq C_{z_{0}}\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

and further for
\omega\in B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbbm{C}\;:\;|z-z_{0}|<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathring{S}
there exists
C_{\varepsilon,z_{0}}\geq 0
such that

(98)

\norm{G''(\omega, y)}_{1}\leq C_{\varepsilon,z_{0}}\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}\,.

We will only show that given
\omega
as above,

(99)

\norm{\log^2(N + \1) (N + \1)^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}} y (N + \1)^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}} y (N + \1)^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}}_{1}\leq\tilde{C}_{\varepsilon,z_{0}}\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}\,.

Using the same reasoning for the other terms of
Equation

97
and
Equation

98
in combination with triangle inequality immediately gives the claims. Note first that we can reduce
k(\omega)
to its real part since the imaginary part only produces a unitary
(N+\mathbbm{1})^{i\imaginary(k(\omega))}
that can be absorbed into the norm. We call the real part
r(\omega)
for now. Since
\omega\in B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})\subset\mathring{S}
we find a
\delta_{\varepsilon}>0
independent of
\omega
, such that
|r(\omega)-k_{1}|<\delta_{\varepsilon}
or more precisely
r(\omega)-k_{1}\leq-\delta_{\varepsilon}
. Hence using Hölder’s inequality, we can deduce

\displaystyle\norm{\log^2(N + \1) (N + \1)^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}} y (N + \1)^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}} y (N + \1)^{\frac{k(\omega)}{4}}}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\log^2(N + \1) (N + \1)^{\frac{r(\omega) - k_1}{4}}}_{\infty}\norm{(N + \1)^{\frac{r(\omega) - k_1}{4}}}_{\infty}\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\log^2(N + \1) (N + \1)^{-\frac{\delta_\varepsilon}{4}}}_{\infty}\norm{(N + \1)^{-\frac{\delta_\varepsilon}{4}}}_{\infty}\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\log^2(N + \1) (N + \1)^{-\frac{\delta_\varepsilon}{4}}}_{\infty}\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

where we used that
x\mapsto e^{kx}
for
k\geq 0
is monotone and further that
(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}}{4}}
is a contraction. Lastly, we have that
x\mapsto\frac{\log^{2}(x+1)}{(x+1)^{\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}}{4}}}
is a bounded function for
x\geq 0
with a bound we call
\tilde{C}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
. This allows us to estimate
\norm{\log^2(N + \1) (N + \1)^{-\frac{\delta_\varepsilon}{4}}}_{\infty}\leq\tilde{C}_{\delta_{\varepsilon}}
, which concludes
Equation

99
and therefore also
Equation

97
and
Equation

98
.

For a general
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
and
z_{0}\in\mathring{S}

Equation

97
allows us to conclude that
G^{\prime}_{1}(z_{0},y)\in\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
is well defined. Further, for
z\in B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})
and
(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{f}
with
y_{n}\to y
in
W^{k_{1},1}
, we have for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}

(100)

\displaystyle\norm{\frac{G_1(z, y_n) - G_1(z_0, y_n)}{z - z_0} - G_1'(z_0, y_n)}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|z-z_{0}|}\int\limits_{[z_{0},z]}\norm{G''_1(\omega, y_n)}_{1}|(\omega-z_{0})d\omega|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\varepsilon,z_{0}}|z-z_{0}|\norm{y_n}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

where we used the expansion and
Equation

98
. Now we can take the limit
n\to\infty
on both sides, as all objects involved are stable w.r.t. that limit (using Lemma
D.1
and
Equation

97
). We get

(101)

\norm{\frac{G_1(z, y) - G_1(z_0, y)}{z - z_0} - G_1'(z_0, y)}_{1}\leq C_{\varepsilon,z_{0}}|z-z_{0}|\norm{y}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

which immediately lets us deduce holomorphy of
G_{1}(\cdot,y):S\to\mathcal{T}_{1,\operatorname{sa}}
on
\mathring{S}
for
y\in W^{k_{1},1}
.

For
G_{2}:S\to W^{k_{1},1}
the holomorphy immediately follows from the fact that
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, which again allows reducing the analysis to a finite-dimensional subspace by taking a cut-off in the Fock basis again. Lastly, we have that
T(G_{2}(z))\in W^{k_{1},1}
for all
z\in S
, which finally gives us that for
z_{0}\in\mathring{S}
and for
z\in B_{\varepsilon}(z_{0})\subset\mathring{S}

\displaystyle\norm{\frac{G(z) - G(z_0)}{z - z_0} - (G_1'(z_0, T(G_2(z_0))) + G_1(z_0, T\{G_2'(z_0)\})}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{\frac{G_1(z, T(G_2(z_0))) - G_1(z_0, T(G_2(z_0)))}{z - z_0} - G_1'(z_0, T(G_2(z_0)))}_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\norm{T}_{W^{k_{1},1}\to W^{k_{1},1}}\norm{\frac{G_2(z) - G_2(z_0)}{z - z_0} - G'_2(z_0)}_{W^{k_{1},1}}

where we used linearity of
G_{1}(z,\cdot)
,
G_{1}^{\prime}(z,\cdot)
and
T
. In addition, we used the bound on
G_{1}(z,\cdot)
from
Equation

96
and
G^{\prime}_{2}
to denote the derivative of
G_{2}
. Now the differentiability of
G_{1}(\cdot,y)
and
G_{2}
at
z_{0}
immediately gives the differentiability of
G
at
z_{0}
, which concludes the proof as
z_{0}\in\mathring{S}
was arbitrary.
∎

Appendix E
Technical lemmas for the generation theorem

Lemma E.1

For
d\geq 0
and
\varepsilon>0
, define the operator

\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{T}_{f}\to\mathcal{T}_{f},\qquad x\mapsto\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon}(x)\coloneqq-\varepsilon\{(N+\mathbbm{1})^{4d},x\}\,.

For
\lambda\geq 0
, we have that
\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{T}_{f}\to\mathcal{T}_{f}
is bijective. While for all
k\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
and
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
one further has

(1)

\displaystyle\norm{(\lambda- \cI_{d, \varepsilon})^{-1}(x)}_{W^{k,1}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda+2\varepsilon}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,;

(2)

\displaystyle\norm{\cI_{d, \varepsilon} \circ(\lambda- \cI_{d, \varepsilon})^{-1}(x)}_{W^{k,1}}\leq{2}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

Proof.

For
\lambda\geq 0
define the following linear operator

\displaystyle(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon})^{-1}:\mathcal{T}_{f}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}_{f},

\displaystyle x=\sum\limits_{\text{finite}}x_{nm}\outerproduct{n}{m}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon})^{-1}(x)\coloneqq\sum\limits_{\text{finite}}x_{nm}\frac{1}{\varepsilon(n+1)^{4d}+\varepsilon(m+1)^{4d}+\lambda}\outerproduct{n}{m}\,.

or alternatively

\displaystyle(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon})^{-1}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(\varepsilon(N+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda/2)s}xe^{-(\varepsilon(N+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda/2)s}ds

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{\text{finite}}e^{-(\varepsilon(n+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda/2)s}x_{nm}e^{-(\varepsilon(m+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda/2)s}\outerproduct{n}{m}ds\,.

The integral representation allows us to deduce that
(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon})^{-1}
preserves positivity. Using the first expression it is straightforward to show that this map is indeed the inverse to
\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon}:\mathcal{T}_{f}\to\mathcal{T}_{f}
. The bound
\norm{(\lambda- \cI_{d, \varepsilon})^{-1} x}_{W^{k,1}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda+2\varepsilon}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}
can be shown, using the integral representation and Hölder inequality:

\displaystyle\norm{(\lambda- \cI_{d, \varepsilon})^{-1}(x)}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\norm{e^{-\varepsilon(N + \1)^{4d} + \lambda/2)s} (N + \1)^{k/4} x (N + \1)^{k/4} e^{-\varepsilon(N + \1)^{4d} + \lambda/2)s}}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\norm{e^{-\varepsilon(N + \1)^{4d} + \lambda/2)s}}_{\infty}^{2}ds\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(2\varepsilon+\lambda)s}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}=\frac{1}{\lambda+2\varepsilon}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

Issues arising from the unbounded nature of
N
can be ignored in the above estimations, as we can take a finite cut-off in the Fock basis due to
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
. For (
2
), we have that on
\mathcal{T}_{f}
,
-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon}\circ(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon})^{-1}=\mathbbm{1}-\lambda(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,\varepsilon})^{-1}
, i.e. for
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}

(102)

\norm{(\1 - \lambda(\lambda- \cI_{d, \varepsilon})^{-1})x}_{W^{k,1}}=\norm{-\cI_{d, \varepsilon}\circ(\lambda- \cI_{d, \varepsilon})^{-1} (x)}_{W^{k,1}}

where the LHS can be upper bounded by
(1+\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+2\varepsilon})\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\leq 2\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}
using (
1
). This proves the last claim.
∎

Lemma E.2

For
p\in\mathbbm{C}[X,Y]
a polynomial of degree
d
and

A:\mathcal{H}_{f}\to\mathcal{H},\qquad\ket{\psi}=\sum\limits_{\text{finite}}\psi_{n}\ket{n}\mapsto p(a,a^{\dagger})\ket{\psi}=\sum\limits_{\text{finite}}\psi_{n}p(a,a^{\dagger})\ket{n}\,,

we get that for all
k\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
and
d^{\prime}\geq d

\displaystyle B_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{f}\to\mathcal{H},\qquad\ket{\psi}\mapsto(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}A(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d^{\prime}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle B_{2}:\mathcal{H}_{f}\to\mathcal{H},\qquad\ket{\psi}\mapsto(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d^{\prime}}A(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}\ket{\psi}

are bounded and therefore can be uniquely extended to a bounded map on
\mathcal{H}
, with the same bound.

Proof.

Since the proof for
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are almost completely analogous, we will only show it here for
B_{1}
. The canonical commutation relation allows us to rewrite
A
as a finite linear combination of monomials of the form
(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}
and
a^{i}N^{j}
with
i+j/2\leq d
. Now by triangle inequality for the norm on
\mathcal{H}
and since the sum of these monomials comprising
A
are finite, for the claim to be true it suffices to show that

(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d}:\mathcal{H}_{f}\to\mathcal{H},\qquad(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}a^{i}N^{j}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d}:\mathcal{H}_{f}\to\mathcal{H}

are bounded, and hence can be uniquely extended to a bounded map on
\mathcal{H}
. We only give the argument for the first map, since it is almost completely analogous to the second one. Let
\ket{\psi}=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M}\psi_{n}\ket{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{f}
, then

\displaystyle\ket{\varphi}\coloneqq(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d^{\prime}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M}\psi_{n}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d^{\prime}}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M}\psi_{n}\frac{(n+1+j)^{k}}{(n+1)^{k}}\frac{n^{j}\prod_{l=1}^{i}\sqrt{n+l}}{(n+1)^{d^{\prime}}}\ket{n+i}\,.

Hence

\displaystyle\norm{\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M}\frac{(n+1+j)^{2k}}{(n+1)^{2k}}\frac{n^{2j}\prod_{l=1}^{i}(n+l)}{(n+1)^{2d^{\prime}}}|\psi_{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M}\frac{(n+1+d)^{2k}}{(n+1)^{2k}}\frac{(n+d)^{2j}(n+d)^{i}}{(n+1)^{2d}}|\psi_{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{n=0}^{M}d^{2k}d^{2d}|\psi_{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle=d^{2(k+d)}\norm{\psi}^{2}\,,

where we used that
i+j/2\leq d
and
d\leq d^{\prime}
. Hence
(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k}(a^{\dagger})^{i}N^{j}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k-d^{\prime}}:\mathcal{H}_{f}\to\mathcal{H}
is bounded by
d^{k+d}
and can be uniquely extended to a bounded linear map on
\mathcal{H}
. This concludes the claim.
∎

Lemma E.3

Let
K\in\mathbbm{N}
. For
i=1,\ldots,K
let
p_{i,1},p_{i,2}\in\mathbbm{C}[X,Y]
polynomials of degree
d_{i,1}
,
d_{i,2}
such that

\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{T}_{f}\to\mathcal{T}_{f},\qquad x\mapsto\mathcal{A}(x)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}A_{i,1}xA_{i,2}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}p_{i,1}(a,a^{\dagger})x\,p_{i,2}(a,a^{\dagger})

where the action of
p_{1/2}(a,a^{\dagger})
on
x
is defined via the action of
a
and
a^{\dagger}
on
\outerproduct{n}{m}
. We then have that for all
k\geq 0
,
d\geq\max\limits_{i=1,\ldots,K}\max\{d_{i,1},d_{i,2}\}
there exists
C_{k}\geq 0
, s.t. for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
\forall x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}

\norm{\cA(x)}_{W^{k,1}}\leq\varepsilon\norm{\{(N + \1)^{4d}, x\}}_{W^{k,1}}+\frac{C_{k}}{\varepsilon}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

Proof.

Let
k\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
. The first step is to show that there exists
c_{k}\geq 0
, s.t. for all
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}

(103)

\norm{\cA(x)}_{W^{k,1}}\leq c_{k}\norm{(N + \1)^d x (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

The argument reduces to showing that for
i=1,\ldots,K
and
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}

\norm{(N + \1)^{k/4} A_{i, 1} x A_{i, 2} (N + \1)^{k/4}}_{1}\leq c_{i,k}\norm{(N + \1)^d x (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}

since the sum comprising
\mathcal{A}
is finite. Note that the trace norm on the LHS is the one on the trace-class operators since the argument might not necessarily be self-adjoint. For
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, we have

\displaystyle\norm{(N + \1)^{k/4} A_{i, 1} x A_{i, 2} (N + \1)^{k/4}}_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\|(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}A_{i,1}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k/4-d}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4+d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4+d}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k/4-d}A_{i,2}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\norm{(N + \1)^{k/4} A_{i, 1} (N + \1)^{-k/4 - d}}_{\infty}\norm{x}_{W^{k+4d,1}}\norm{(N + \1)^{-k/4 - d} A_{i, 2} (N + \1)^{k/4}}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq c_{i,k}\norm{(N + \1)^d x (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}\,

where we used Lemma
E.2
to argue that the operators involved are bounded and we can employ Hölder’s inequality to split them off. Subsequently, we replaced the operator norms with the constant
c_{i,k}
. Now in the second step we show that for all
\varepsilon>0
and
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}

\norm{(N + \1)^d x (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}\leq\varepsilon\norm{\{(N + \1)^{4d}, x\}}_{W^{k,1}}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

Combining this with
Equation

103
then immediately provides the claim.
Therefore, let
\lambda>0
and
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
with
x\geq 0
. We find

\displaystyle\norm{(N + 1)^d(\lambda- \cI_{d, 1})^{-1}(x) (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\tr[(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,1})^{-1}\{(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4+d}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4+d}\}]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}\tr[e^{-(2(N+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda)s}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{2d}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}]ds

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\tr[\int\limits_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(2(N+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda)s}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{2d}ds\,(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\tr[\frac{(N+\mathbbm{1})^{2d}}{(N+\mathbbm{1})^{4d}+\lambda}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}]\,,

where we used the map
\mathcal{I}_{d,1}
from Lemma
E.1
, the integral representation of its resolvent
(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,1})^{-1}
and that this resolvent preserves positivity. Further, we applied the cyclicity of the trace and conveniently suppressed issues that might arise from the unbounded nature of
N
by taking a cut-off in the Fock basis. This is justified by
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
. Now we can use that
(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}\geq 0
to bound the RHS of the above chain of inequalities to get

\displaystyle\norm{(N + 1)^d(\lambda- \cI_{d, 1})^{-1}(x) (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}\tr[(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}]

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

For a general
x\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
, we can set
y=(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}x(N+\mathbbm{1})^{k/4}
decompose into
y=y_{+}-y_{-}
the positive and negative part of
y
respectively and then set
x_{\pm}=(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k/4}y_{\pm}(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k/4}
. We clearly have that
x_{\pm}\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
,
x=x_{+}-x_{-}
and
x_{\pm}\geq 0
as
(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k/4}\cdot(N+\mathbbm{1})^{-k/4}
preserves positivity. This allows us to apply what we have shown above to obtain

\displaystyle\|(N+1)^{d}(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,1})^{-1}

\displaystyle(x)(N+\mathbbm{1})^{d}\|_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{(N + 1)^d(\lambda- \cI_{d, 1})^{-1}(x_+) (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\norm{(N + 1)^d(\lambda- \cI_{d, 1})^{-1}(x_-) (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}(\norm{x_+}_{W^{k,1}}+\norm{x_-}_{W^{k,1}})

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}(\norm{y_+}_{1}+\norm{y_-}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}\norm{y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}

Lastly we can use the bijectivity of
(\lambda-\mathcal{I}_{d,1})
on
\mathcal{T}_{f}
(q.v. Lemma
E.1
) and triangle inequality to conclude

(104)

\norm{(N + \1)^d x (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}\leq\sup\limits_{s\geq 0}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}\norm{\cI_{d, 1}(x)}_{W^{k,1}}+\lambda\sup\limits_{s\geq 0}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}\,.

Choosing
\lambda=\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}
, we find that
\sup\limits_{s\geq 1}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\lambda}<\varepsilon
, hence

(105)

\norm{(N + \1)^d x (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}\leq\varepsilon\norm{\{(N +\1)^{4d}, x\}}_{W^{k,1}}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon}\norm{x}_{W^{k,1}}

∎

Lemma E.4

(Interpolation Lemma)

Let
k_{0}<k_{1}\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
and
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
an operator on
W^{k_{j},1}
,
j=0,1
respectively. Further, assume that the closure of
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
defines a strongly continuous semigroup
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{j})_{t\geq 0}
with

(106)

\norm{\cP_t^{j}}_{W^{k_{j},1}\to W^{k_{j},1}}\leq M_{j}e^{\omega_{j}t}\quad\forall t\geq 0\,

in both spaces, respectively. Then for
\theta\in[0,1]
and
k_{\theta}=\theta k_{1}+(1-\theta)k_{0}
the following are true

1.

The closure of
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
defines a strongly continuous semigroup on
W^{k_{\theta},1}
with

(107)

\norm{\cP^\theta_t}_{W^{k_{\theta},1}\to W^{k_{\theta},1}}\leq M_{0}^{1-\theta}M_{1}^{\theta}e^{(\omega_{k_{0}}(1-\theta)+\omega_{k_{1}}\theta)t}\quad\forall t\geq 0\,.

2.

(\mathcal{P}^{\theta}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
agrees with
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{j})_{t\geq 0}
on
W^{k_{j},1}\cap W^{k_{\theta},1}
for
j=1,2
.

Proof.

We begin with the second claim and only cover
k_{j}=k_{0}
as the other case only requires minor changes that are left to the reader. Let
\theta\in(0,1)
and the closure of
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
the generator of
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{0})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta})_{t\geq 0}
on the respective spaces. Since
k_{0}<k_{\theta}
and hence
W^{k_{\theta},1}\Subset W^{k_{0},1}
, we have that the closure of
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
in
W^{k_{\theta},1}
agrees with the restriction of the closure in
W^{k_{0},1}
. As the semigroup is completely determined by its generator, we find that the semigroups agree on
W^{k_{\theta},1}
.

For the first claim, note that the semigroups
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{j})_{t\geq 0}
,
j=1,2
agree on
W^{k_{1},1}
by
Item

2
, which allows us to employ the Stein-Weiss theorem for Bosonic Sobolev spaces (Theorem
2.14
) to conclude
Equation

107
. It remains to check that the families of bounded maps
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta})_{t\geq 0}
are strongly continuous semigroups generated by the closure of
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
on
W^{k_{\theta},1}
. We have that
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\theta}=\mathbbm{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta}\mathcal{P}_{s}^{\theta}=\mathcal{P}_{t+1}^{\theta}

\forall t,s\geq 0
as a consequence of
\mathcal{P}_{t}^{0}|_{W^{k_{\theta},1}}=\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta}

\forall t
(this equality holds by Theorem
2.14
). The strong continuity follows, due to
\mathcal{P}^{1}_{t}=\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta}|_{W^{k_{1},1}}
, as for
x\in W^{k_{1},1}

\lim\limits_{t\to 0}\norm{\cP_t^\theta(x) - x}_{W^{k_{\theta},1}}=\lim\limits_{t\to 0}\norm{\cP^1_t(x) - x}_{W^{k_{\theta},1}}\leq\lim\limits_{t\to 0}\norm{\cP^1_t(x) - x}_{W^{k_{1},1}}=0

where we used
W^{k_{1},1}\Subset W^{k_{\theta},1}
and that
\mathcal{P}^{1}_{t}
is a strongly continous semigroup on
W^{k_{1},1}
. For general
x\in W^{k_{\theta},1}
, we find
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset W^{k_{\theta},1}
converging to
x
in
W^{k_{\theta},1}
and for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\to 0}\norm{\cP_t^\theta(x) - x}_{W^{k_{\theta},1}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim\limits_{t\to 0}\left[(1+M_{0}^{1-\theta}M_{1}^{\theta}e^{(\omega_{k_{0}}(1-\theta)+\omega_{k_{1}}\theta)t})\norm{x - x_n}_{W^{k_{\theta},1}}+\norm{\cP_t^\theta(x_n) - x_n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(1+M_{0}^{1-\theta}M_{1}^{\theta})\norm{x - x_n}_{W^{k_{\theta},1}}

which concludes the strong continuity. It remains to argue that the closure of
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
on
W^{k_{\theta},1}
is indeed the generator of
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta})_{t\geq 0}
. By
[
23
, Sec. II.2.3]
, we find that the restriction of the generator
(\hat{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{D}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}))
of
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta})_{t\geq 0}
to
W^{k_{1},1}
is the generator of
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{1})_{t\geq 0}
with
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
being a core for this restricted generator on
W^{k_{1},1}
by assumption. This in particular means that for
\lambda>\omega_{k}
,
\lambda-\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L})\to W^{k_{1},1}
has a dense range in
W^{k_{1},1}
, which further allows us to conclude that
\lambda-\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L})\to W^{k_{\theta},1}
has a dense range in
W^{k_{\theta},1}
. Now as it is
\omega_{k_{\theta}}
-quasi dissipative (being the restriction of the generator of the semigroup
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{\theta})_{t\geq 0}
) we can conclude that indeed
(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L}))
is closable in
W^{k_{\theta},1}
with the closure
(\hat{\mathcal{L}},\mathcal{D}(\hat{\mathcal{L}}))
(c.f.
[
23
, Proposition 3.14]
).
∎

Lemma E.5

(Approximation Lemma)

Let
K\in\mathbbm{N}
. For
i=1,\ldots,K
let
p_{i,1},p_{i,2}\in\mathbbm{C}[X,Y]
polynomials of degree
d_{i,1}
,
d_{i,2}
and
\{a_{i,n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathbbm{C}
convergent sequences with limits
a_{i}\in\mathbbm{C}
such that
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n})=\mathcal{T}_{f})\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
is an operator sequence with

(108)

\mathcal{A}_{n}:\mathcal{T}_{f}\to\mathcal{T}_{f},\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{n}(x)\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}a_{i,n}A_{i,1}\,x\,A_{i,2}\coloneqq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}a_{i,n}p_{i,1}(a,a^{\dagger})\,x\,p_{i,2}(a,a^{\dagger})\,

with
A_{i,1}=p_{i,1}(a,a^{\dagger})
and
A_{i,2}=p_{i,2}(a,a^{\dagger})
(see Lemma
E.3
). If for all
k\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
there exists
M_{k},\omega_{k}
such that for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}
the closure of
(\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n}))
generates a strongly continuous semigroup
(\mathcal{P}_{t}^{n})_{t\geq 0}
on
W^{k,1}
with

(109)

\norm{\cP_t^n}_{W^{k,1}\to W^{k,1}}\leq M_{k}e^{\omega_{k}t}\quad\forall t\in\mathbbm{R}\,,

then the closure of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{T}_{f})
, the pointwise limit of
(\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n}))
, defines a strongly continuous semigroup on
W^{k,1}
for
k\geq 0
as well. We further get that the semigroups generated by the closure of
(\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n}))
converge uniformly (in time) on compact intervals to the semigroup generated by the closure of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
and that
Equation

109
also holds for the limiting semigroup.

Proof.

Let
k\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
. To prove the lemma, we first note that
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
is densely defined and the pointwise limit of
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n}))\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
. To employ the second Trotter-Kato approximation theorem, which implies the claim (see the version in
[
23
, Thm. III.4.9]
), we need to show that there exists
\lambda>0
such that
(\lambda-\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
has dense range in
W^{k,1}
. We will do so by showing that the closure of the range contains
\mathcal{T}_{f}
which is a dense subset of
W^{k,1}
. Therefore let
\lambda>\max\{\omega_{k},\omega_{k+4d}\}
(with
\omega_{\cdot}
from
Equation

109
and
d
the maximal degree of the polynomials but at least one, i.e.
d=\max\limits_{i=1,\ldots,K}\max\{d_{i,1},d_{i,2},1\}
). By assumption, we have that for all
n\in\mathbbm{N}
the operator
(\lambda-\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n}))
has dense range in
W^{k+4d,1}
, meaning in particular that
for any
\xi\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
we find a sequence
\{x_{n,m}\}_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}
which is convergent in
W^{k+4d,1}
and further

(110)

\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}\norm{(\lambda- \cA_n)(x_{n, m}) - \xi}_{W^{k+4d,1}}=0\,.

In addition, we can choose the sequence such that for all
m\in\mathbbm{N}
,
\norm{(\lambda- \cA_n)(x_{n, m})}_{W^{k+4d,1}}\leq\norm{\xi}_{W^{k+4d,1}}+1
. Due to the
\omega_{k+4d}
-quasi dissipativity of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
(it is a generator of a strongly continuous semigroup with a bound given in
Equation

109
) this immediately implies
\norm{x_{n, m}}_{W^{k+4d,1}}\leq M_{k+4d}\frac{\norm{\xi}_{W^{k+4d,1}}+1}{\lambda-\omega_{k+4d}}=:c_{\xi}
, i.e. the set
\{x_{n,m}\}_{n,m\in\mathbbm{N}}
is bounded in
W^{k+4d,1}
. We now have that for
n,m\in\mathbbm{N}

(111)

\displaystyle\norm{(\lambda- \cA)(x_{n, m}) - \xi}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{(\lambda- \cA_n)(x_{n, m}) - \xi}_{W^{k,1}}+\norm{(\cA- \cA_n)(x_{n, m})}_{W^{k,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{(\lambda- \cA_n)(x_{n, m}) - \xi}_{W^{k,1}}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}c_{i,k}|a_{i}-a_{i,n}|\norm{x_{n, m}}_{W^{k+4d,1}}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{(\lambda- \cA_n)(x_{n, m}) - \xi}_{W^{k,1}}+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}|a_{i}-a_{i,n}|c_{i,k}c_{\xi}

\displaystyle\leq\norm{(\lambda- \cA_n)(x_{n, m}) - \xi}_{W^{k,1}}+C\sum\limits_{i=1}^{K}|a_{i}-a_{i,n}|\,.

In the first line we used triangle inequality, in the second one the explicit form of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}
and then that there exists
c_{k,i}\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\norm{(N + \1)^{k/4}A_{i, 1} x_{n, m} A_{i, 2} (N + \1)^{k/4}}_{1}\leq c_{k,i}\norm{(N + \1)^d x_{n, m} (N + \1)^d}_{W^{k,1}}

as in the proof of Lemma
E.3
. Lastly we used the uniform bound
c_{\xi}
and set
C=\max_{i=1,\ldots,K}c_{i,k}c_{\xi}
. By a proper choice of a subsequence of
\{x_{n,m}\}_{n,m\in\mathbbm{N}}
, we get that the RHS of
Equation

111
vanishes. Since
\{x_{n,m}\}_{n,m\in\mathbbm{N}}
is bounded in
W^{k+4d,1}
it is in particular precompact in
W^{k,1}
(as of the compact embedding of the Sobolev spaces), meaning we can further choose the aforementioned sequence to be convergent in
W^{k,1}
. Let us call it
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{f}
. To summarise, for the chosen
\lambda
and
\xi\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
arbitrary we have constructed a sequence
\{y_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
which is convergent in
W^{k,1}
and further
\{(\lambda-\mathcal{A})(y_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbbm{N}}
converges to
\xi
in
W^{k,1}
. Hence the closure of the range of
(\lambda-\mathcal{A},\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}))
contains
\mathcal{T}_{f}
a dense subset of
W^{k,1}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark
.

In the above lemma, it suffices to assume that the semigroups are Sobolev preserving, as one can interpolate between the sequence elements to obtain semigroups for
k\in\mathbbm{R}_{+}
.
